What a run is. One invocation of the framework on one design with one mode: here raygentop with sop_2_mode.

What happened. 2 rewrites measured (Pass 1, Pass 2); kept: Pass 2 (the lowest area x delay, a later rewrite having to beat it by more than 4.60%). Loop stopped by: planner declined (no representative mapping).

Result. Area x delay -2.62% against the unmodified design on the same layout (koios_extra_small, 90 x 90): 2.24585e+07 vs 2.30616e+07 (baseline: median per term over the entry's seed runs (its seed_medians; seeds lists them)); verification: not recorded in the run record.

Mode contract

recorded (outcome.json contract field)

Verbatim, as recorded with the run and sent to the agents.

- primitive to instantiate: `int_sop_2` (precision INT19)
- computes: out = ax*ay + bx*by + chainin
- input ports: clk[1] (clock), reset[1], mode_sigs[12], ax[18], ay[19], bx[18], by[19], chainin[37]
- output ports: result[37], chainout[37]
- arithmetic: the block reads each operand port as an unsigned number, and its result is unsigned. An operand narrower than its port is zero-extended to the port width. The result wraps at 37 bits: a value past the result width keeps its low 37 bits (modulo 2^37), with no saturation and no flag.
- registers: on `clk`, the block registers its operands (`ax`, `ay`, `bx`, `by`) at one clock edge and `result` at the next, with the arithmetic between the two registers: operands applied before clock edge k appear in `result` after edge k+1, 2 cycles later. The block has no clock enable and no path around either register. `chainin` is registered at the block's entry like an operand and reaches `result` 2 cycles after it is applied. `chainout` is combinational from the input registers: it carries the value `result` loads at the next clock edge unless that edge clears it, one cycle before `result` shows it.
- reset: `reset` is synchronous and active high: at a clock edge that samples it high it clears every register of the block (the operand registers, the chainin register and the result register) to 0, and the block loads again at the next edge that samples it low.
- power-up: the block's registers have no initial value. Each loads its input at every clock edge where `reset` is low, so, for a block whose inputs, `chainin` included, are defined, the result is defined from the second edge after power-up, with or without a reset. A rewrite may not rely on the result before then.
- tie-off: `chainin` = 0 when the block is not chained (a dedicated chain input: only another block's `chainout` or a constant may drive it); `mode_sigs` = 0 always (the architecture gives the port no meaning for this mode).
- connections: connect every input port, by the port names above; a product-term port the rewrite does not use is tied to 0. `chainout` reaches only the next block's `chainin`, through a dedicated connection to the block directly below it in the column; it has no connection to general routing, so nothing else may read it.

9 call(s) in 3 passes, in order

Role: planner plan reviewer editor acceptance reviewer. A plan reviewer sending the plan back, or the editor being asked again, is a retry inside the same pass.

prompt
Task

You are the planner. Read the design source, raygentop.v, and decide which of the candidate arithmetic regions listed below to rewrite onto the hard block described next, or that none should be rewritten. Write a plan for the editor, who carries it out; do not edit any file.

The block
  • primitive to instantiate: int_sop_2 (precision INT19)
  • computes: out = ax*ay + bx*by + chainin
  • input ports: clk[1] (clock), reset[1], mode_sigs[12], ax[18], ay[19], bx[18], by[19], chainin[37]
  • output ports: result[37], chainout[37]
  • arithmetic: the block reads each operand port as an unsigned number, and its result is unsigned. An operand narrower than its port is zero-extended to the port width. The result wraps at 37 bits: a value past the result width keeps its low 37 bits (modulo 2^37), with no saturation and no flag.
  • registers: on clk, the block registers its operands (ax, ay, bx, by) at one clock edge and result at the next, with the arithmetic between the two registers: operands applied before clock edge k appear in result after edge k+1, 2 cycles later. The block has no clock enable and no path around either register. chainin is registered at the block's entry like an operand and reaches result 2 cycles after it is applied. chainout is combinational from the input registers: it carries the value result loads at the next clock edge unless that edge clears it, one cycle before result shows it.
  • reset: reset is synchronous and active high: at a clock edge that samples it high it clears every register of the block (the operand registers, the chainin register and the result register) to 0, and the block loads again at the next edge that samples it low.
  • power-up: the block's registers have no initial value. Each loads its input at every clock edge where reset is low, so, for a block whose inputs, chainin included, are defined, the result is defined from the second edge after power-up, with or without a reset. A rewrite may not rely on the result before then.
  • tie-off: chainin = 0 when the block is not chained (a dedicated chain input: only another block's chainout or a constant may drive it); mode_sigs = 0 always (the architecture gives the port no meaning for this mode).
  • connections: connect every input port, by the port names above; a product-term port the rewrite does not use is tied to 0. chainout reaches only the next block's chainin, through a dedicated connection to the block directly below it in the column; it has no connection to general routing, so nothing else may read it.
The block on this architecture

These are read from the architecture file.

element on a pathns
into the block: a path ends at the block's input register (setup)0.019
inside the block: input register clock-to-Q + delay + output register setup0.060 + 2.140 + 0.019 = 2.219
out of the block: a path starts at the output register of result (clock-to-Q)0.060
chainout: input register clock-to-Q + delay, to the output pin0.060 + 2.140 = 2.200
soft flip-flop, clock to output0.060
  • Blocks in series are separate stages. A path through chainout starts at an input register and continues, in the same stage, into what it drives.
  • A block of this mode takes a DSP tile alone.
  • The block has chain ports: a block's chainout can drive the next block's chainin directly, so a sum can continue from block to block.
  • The device has 132 DSP tiles.
  • One DSP tile's area is 253779 minimum-width transistor areas and one CLB's 27905.
The design
Candidate regions

An id names a product by its module and its multiply. One that ends in (sum_in=<module>) is a product whose sum sits in that other module: the product leaves its own module through a port and is added there, so the arithmetic to convert spans both. A #1, #2 after an id separates instances of the same product that sit in sums of different shapes.

In every row, operation: sop.
In every row, instances: 1.
In every row, register stages between the products and their sum: products registered 1 stage(s) before the sum.
In every row, products in the whole sum this one belongs to: 3 products in one chain of adders.

Correctness: these must hold
  • Same behavior. The rewritten design must produce the same outputs as the original on every cycle, with every register of the original starting at 0. Do not change what the design computes. Logic added only to reproduce it (recomputing a value, deriving the block's reset, correcting a block's result) is allowed.
  • Visibility. Only what the block's output ports carry is visible outside it. A value read by anything else (another expression, a module port, another module) cannot go inside a block: keep it outside, or recompute it. Check the source for every reader.
  • Terms. Keep every term of the original arithmetic. A term with no matching block port is computed outside the block. A port the design ties to a constant may be tied the same way on the block.
  • Registers and reset. The block's registers, if it has any, behave as the contract states. A design register may become a block register only if that keeps its behavior. Drive the block's reset so that "same behavior" holds after every reset, not only at start-up. Registers the rewrite adds start unknown; do not rely on their initial value.
  • Assumptions. If the rewrite is correct only under an assumption about the design's inputs or use (a bound on a value, when a signal is asserted), state it on a line starting ASSUMES: and cite the lines of the design that support it. The reference testbench does not know your assumption, so the rewrite must still match the original on the testbench's stimulus, which does not respect it.
  • Scope. Change only what the rewrite onto the block needs.
Objective

Minimize area × delay against the original design. Among rewrites that lower it, choose the one that lowers it most.

Output
Tiers
  • T1: The block replaces the region's arithmetic as it stands: the same operations on the same operands, now inside the block.
  • T2: The region's arithmetic is regrouped to fit the block (products regrouped into the block's sums, registers moved), computing the same values.
  • NONE: You propose no rewrite this time. The plan states why, from what you were given: no correct rewrite, or none expected to lower area × delay, or the best result so far is the best available. The run then ends and keeps the best result so far if its median over every seed is below the original's, and the original design otherwise (also when no rewrite has beaten it).
Header

Write exactly this header, each key at the start of its own line, then the plan:

TIER: <exactly one of T1, T2, NONE>
REGIONS: <the converted candidate ids, comma-separated; NONE when TIER is NONE>
SHARED_REDUCTION: <the signal or module the converted products are summed in; NONE for a single region>
BLOCKS: <how many blocks the rewrite instantiates, one integer>
MAPPING: <which design signals go to which block ports>
PLAN: <the plan, stating everything listed below>

What the plan must state
  • Every candidate region, converted or left, with the reason each is left; candidates that share a reason may be grouped.
  • How many blocks the rewrite adds.
  • For each converted region, the register stages from its operands to its result, before and after the rewrite.
Output rules
  • Write nothing before the TIER line; read the source first.
  • Your reply is read by the framework, not answered by a person, so do not end with a question or an offer.
  • If a change to the design itself, not to the rewrite, would let more of it use the block, you may say so on a line starting DESIGN_CHANGE:. It is reported to the user and does not change your decision.
Reference simulation

After the edit, the framework simulates the edited design against the original with the reference testbench, comparing every output of bilinearintrp on every cycle after the testbench's lead-in phase (its first 8 cycles). A candidate that does not match is not measured. The testbench (tb_equiv_bilin.v) and the block's behavioural model (int_sop_2.v) are in sim_reference/.

Read these files in this directory: raygentop.v

response

I'll start by reading the design source around the candidate regions.

TIER: T2
REGIONS: bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2874$348, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2875$349, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2876$350, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2877$351, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2878$352, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2879$353, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2880$354, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2881$355, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2882$356
SHARED_REDUCTION: the three sums in bilinearintrp: r (i1r+i2r+i3r), g (i1g+i2g+i3g), b (i1b+i2b+i3b) — two blocks feed each sum
BLOCKS: 6
MAPPING:

  • w is a new combinational wire in bilinearintrp: wire [7:0] w = 8'b11111111 - u - v; (exactly the RHS of line 2862, 8-bit, same wrap). u, v stay as they are: the combinational reg [7:0] of the always @(selectuv or ...) case at lines 2818-2858.
  • Block R_UV: ax={10'b0,u}, ay={12'b0,ru}, bx={10'b0,v}, by={12'b0,rv}, chainin=37'b0, result=r_uv[36:0], chainout unconnected.
  • Block R_W: ax={10'b0,w}, ay={12'b0,rw}, bx=18'b0, by=19'b0, chainin=37'b0, result=r_w[36:0], chainout unconnected.
  • Block G_UV: ax={10'b0,u}, ay={12'b0,gu}, bx={10'b0,v}, by={12'b0,gv}, chainin=37'b0, result=g_uv, chainout unconnected.
  • Block G_W: ax={10'b0,w}, ay={12'b0,gw}, bx=18'b0, by=19'b0, chainin=37'b0, result=g_w, chainout unconnected.
  • Block B_UV: ax={10'b0,u}, ay={12'b0,bu}, bx={10'b0,v}, by={12'b0,bv}, chainin=37'b0, result=b_uv, chainout unconnected.
  • Block B_W: ax={10'b0,w}, ay={12'b0,bw}, bx=18'b0, by=19'b0, chainin=37'b0, result=b_w, chainout unconnected.
  • Every block: clk=clk, reset=1'b0, mode_sigs=12'b0.
  • Outputs: assign r = r_uv[6:0] + r_w[6:0]; assign g = g_uv[6:0] + g_w[6:0]; assign b = b_uv[6:0] + b_w[6:0];

PLAN:

What is being replaced (lines 2860-2886). The clocked block holds two register stages: stage 1 latches the muxed u,v, the value 8'hFF-u-v, and the nine colour weights (ul,vl,wl,rul..bwl); stage 2 latches the nine products (i1r..i3b, each an exact 8×7 → 15-bit product). r,g,b are the combinational 3-product sums truncated to 7 bits. All of ul,vl,wl,rul..bwl,i1r..i3b are local regs read only at lines 2874-2886 (verified by grep over the whole file; the ul/vl at lines 2706-2756 belong to linearmap, a different module), so nothing outside reads a value that moves inside a block — the visibility rule is satisfied.

All nine candidates are converted; none is left. Each colour's 3-product chain becomes two blocks: one block takes the u and v products, the second takes the w product alone with bx=by=0. The two 37-bit results are added in 7 bits outside, which is all r,g,b keep.

Why two blocks per colour and not one chained pair. Chaining would be the natural fit for a 3-product sum, but it does not keep the module's latency. chainout is combinational from the upstream block's input registers, so it is valid one cycle after that block's operands are applied; the downstream block then needs two more cycles from its chainin register to result — three cycles in total, while the original module produces r two cycles after the inputs are sampled. There is no earlier tap to steal a cycle from (u,v are combinational from the module's ports) and the module's output may not be delayed, so chaining is not usable here. Every block therefore runs standalone with chainin tied to 0 and chainout left unconnected (it has no legal reader).

Why not the cheaper 3-block variant. Three blocks (the u/v pair per colour) plus three surviving soft 8×7 multipliers and their wl/rwl/gwl/bwl and i3r/i3g/i3b registers would cost fewer DSP tiles, but it leaves the module's longest register-to-register path — a multiplier in soft logic — exactly as it is in the original, so it can only win on area and never on delay. The 6-block version removes all nine multipliers and all 222 flip-flops of the two register stages from the fabric, leaving only the 6:1 u/v mux, one 8-bit subtract and three 7-bit adds in CLBs; the module's register-to-register path becomes the block's 2.219 ns. Since the objective is area × delay over the whole design, the full conversion is the better bet. 6 of 132 DSP tiles are used, well inside the budget.

Register stages, per converted region (all nine are identical).

  • Before: 2 stages from the module's inputs to the sum — stage 1 = ul/vl/wl and the weight registers (lines 2862-2873), stage 2 = the product registers i1r..i3b (lines 2874-2882), then the sum is combinational into r/g/b.
  • After: 2 stages — stage 1 = the block's operand registers (they take the role of ul/vl/wl and the weight registers), stage 2 = the block's result register (it takes the role of the product registers, with the partial sum of two products now formed inside), then the 7-bit add is combinational into r/g/b. Operands applied before edge k reach result after edge k+1 in both the original and the rewrite, so r,g,b change on exactly the same cycles.

Cycle-exact argument. Let the module's inputs be sampled at edge n-1. Original: ul,rul hold u,ru of that sample during (n-1,n); at edge n i1r = u·ru, i2r = v·rv, i3r = w·rw, so r during (n,n+1) is their sum mod 128. Rewrite: each block's operand registers sample the same combinational u,v,w,ru..bw at edge n-1 and load result at edge n, giving r_uv = u·ru + v·rv and r_w = w·rw over the same interval; r_uv[6:0] + r_w[6:0] equals (i1r+i2r+i3r) mod 128. No wrap concerns: each product is at most 255·127 = 32385 and a block's sum at most 64770, far below 2^37, so the block's modulo-2^37 result is the exact sum, and truncating each result to 7 bits before adding is exact modulo 128.

Reset and power-up. bilinearintrp has no reset port and the testbench drives none, so every block's reset is tied to 1'b0 — there is no design reset whose behaviour has to be reproduced. The blocks' registers are X for the first two clock edges; the testbench's 8-cycle all-zero lead-in ends well after that, and with zero inputs both the original and the rewrite read 0 on r,g,b by then. No register the rewrite adds is relied on at power-up (none is added: the only new logic is combinational).

Edits for the editor, inside module bilinearintrp only.

  1. Delete the reg declarations at lines 2796-2816 (ul,vl,wl,i1b..i3r,rul..bwl). Keep reg [7:0] u; reg [7:0] v; (2794-2795) and the combinational always at 2818-2858 unchanged.
  2. Delete the whole always @(posedge clk) block at lines 2860-2883; it becomes empty.
  3. Add wire [7:0] w = 8'b11111111 - u - v; and six 37-bit wires r_uv, r_w, g_uv, g_w, b_uv, b_w.
  4. Instantiate the six int_sop_2 blocks with the port connections in MAPPING, by name, connecting every input port (clk, reset, mode_sigs, ax, ay, bx, by, chainin) and leaving chainout unconnected.
  5. Replace lines 2884-2886 with the three 7-bit adds in MAPPING.
    No other module and nothing outside bilinearintrp changes.

DESIGN_CHANGE: If bilinearintrp were allowed one extra cycle of latency on r,g,b (or if its u/v/weight inputs arrived one register stage earlier from resultrecieve/rgsramcontroller), each colour's three products would fit two blocks joined by chainout→chainin, removing the three soft 7-bit output adders and putting the whole reduction inside the DSP column; the block count would stay 6.

Read completed /work/sim_reference/int_sop_2.v
input
{
 "file_path": "/work/sim_reference/int_sop_2.v"

output
1	// int_sop_2: result = ax*ay + bx*by + chainin, unsigned, modulo 2^37 (the sum wraps at the
2	// result width).
3	// Ports: clk, reset, mode_sigs[12], ax[18], ay[19], bx[18], by[19], chainin[37] in; result[37],
4	// chainout[37] out. mode_sigs is not read.
5	// Registers (the arch's annotation read literally, decision 2026-10-16): every input is registered
6	// at the block's entry at one clock edge (ax, ay, bx, by and chainin alike); the products
7	// and their sum are formed from those registers and loaded into result at the next edge.
8	// Cycles: every operand and chainin to result 2. chainout is combinational from the input
9	// registers: the sum result loads at the next edge unless that edge clears it (operands and
10	// chainin to chainout 1).
11	// Reset: synchronous, active high (2026-10-17: the arch's annotation does not distinguish a reset
12	// register from a clear sampled by the registers themselves): at a clock edge that samples it high
13	// every register of the block (operand registers, chainin register, result) is cleared to 0; the block
14	// loads again at the next edge that samples it low. No register has an initial value: every one is
15	// X until the first clear.
16	module int_sop_2 (
17	    input clk,
18	    input reset,
19	    input [11:0] mode_sigs,
20	    input [17:0] ax, input [18:0] ay,
21	    input [17:0] bx, input [18:0] by,
22	    input [36:0] chainin,
23	    output reg [36:0] result,
24	    output [36:0] chainout
25	);
26	
27	    // stage 1: the input registers
28	    reg [17:0] ax_reg, bx_reg;
29	    reg [18:0] ay_reg, by_reg;
30	    reg [36:0] chainin_reg;
31	
32	    // combinational middle: the two products and their sum, from the registers
33	    wire [36:0] prod_a = ax_reg * ay_reg;                        // 18x19 -> 37, exact
34	    wire [36:0] prod_b = bx_reg * by_reg;
35	    wire [36:0] sum    = prod_a + prod_b + chainin_reg;          // modulo 2^37
36	
37	    always @(posedge clk) begin
38	        if (reset) begin
39	            result <= 0;
40	            ax_reg <= 0; ay_reg <= 0; bx_reg <= 0; by_reg <= 0; chainin_reg <= 0;
41	        end
42	        else begin
43	            ax_reg <= ax; ay_reg <= ay; bx_reg <= bx; by_reg <= by; chainin_reg <= chainin;
44	            result <= sum;                                       // stage 2
45	        end
46	    end
47	
48	    assign chainout = sum;                                  // the sum of the input registers
49	
50	endmodule

Task

You are the plan reviewer. You did not write this plan. Check it against the design source, the block's contract and the correctness rules; approve it if it violates none.

The block
  • primitive to instantiate: int_sop_2 (precision INT19)
  • computes: out = ax*ay + bx*by + chainin
  • input ports: clk[1] (clock), reset[1], mode_sigs[12], ax[18], ay[19], bx[18], by[19], chainin[37]
  • output ports: result[37], chainout[37]
  • arithmetic: the block reads each operand port as an unsigned number, and its result is unsigned. An operand narrower than its port is zero-extended to the port width. The result wraps at 37 bits: a value past the result width keeps its low 37 bits (modulo 2^37), with no saturation and no flag.
  • registers: on clk, the block registers its operands (ax, ay, bx, by) at one clock edge and result at the next, with the arithmetic between the two registers: operands applied before clock edge k appear in result after edge k+1, 2 cycles later. The block has no clock enable and no path around either register. chainin is registered at the block's entry like an operand and reaches result 2 cycles after it is applied. chainout is combinational from the input registers: it carries the value result loads at the next clock edge unless that edge clears it, one cycle before result shows it.
  • reset: reset is synchronous and active high: at a clock edge that samples it high it clears every register of the block (the operand registers, the chainin register and the result register) to 0, and the block loads again at the next edge that samples it low.
  • power-up: the block's registers have no initial value. Each loads its input at every clock edge where reset is low, so, for a block whose inputs, chainin included, are defined, the result is defined from the second edge after power-up, with or without a reset. A rewrite may not rely on the result before then.
  • tie-off: chainin = 0 when the block is not chained (a dedicated chain input: only another block's chainout or a constant may drive it); mode_sigs = 0 always (the architecture gives the port no meaning for this mode).
  • connections: connect every input port, by the port names above; a product-term port the rewrite does not use is tied to 0. chainout reaches only the next block's chainin, through a dedicated connection to the block directly below it in the column; it has no connection to general routing, so nothing else may read it.
The block on this architecture

These are read from the architecture file.

  • Blocks in series are separate stages. A path through chainout starts at an input register and continues, in the same stage, into what it drives.
  • A block of this mode takes a DSP tile alone.
  • The block has chain ports: a block's chainout can drive the next block's chainin directly, so a sum can continue from block to block.
  • The device has 132 DSP tiles.
  • One DSP tile's area is 253779 minimum-width transistor areas and one CLB's 27905.
The design

The design source is raygentop.v. The candidate regions the planner was offered:

An id names a product by its module and its multiply. One that ends in (sum_in=<module>) is a product whose sum sits in that other module: the product leaves its own module through a port and is added there, so the arithmetic to convert spans both. A #1, #2 after an id separates instances of the same product that sit in sums of different shapes.

In every row, operation: sop.
In every row, instances: 1.
In every row, register stages between the products and their sum: products registered 1 stage(s) before the sum.
In every row, products in the whole sum this one belongs to: 3 products in one chain of adders.

Correctness: these must hold
  • Same behavior. The rewritten design must produce the same outputs as the original on every cycle, with every register of the original starting at 0. Do not change what the design computes. Logic added only to reproduce it (recomputing a value, deriving the block's reset, correcting a block's result) is allowed.
  • Visibility. Only what the block's output ports carry is visible outside it. A value read by anything else (another expression, a module port, another module) cannot go inside a block: keep it outside, or recompute it. Check the source for every reader.
  • Terms. Keep every term of the original arithmetic. A term with no matching block port is computed outside the block. A port the design ties to a constant may be tied the same way on the block.
  • Registers and reset. The block's registers, if it has any, behave as the contract states. A design register may become a block register only if that keeps its behavior. Drive the block's reset so that "same behavior" holds after every reset, not only at start-up. Registers the rewrite adds start unknown; do not rely on their initial value.
  • Assumptions. If the rewrite is correct only under an assumption about the design's inputs or use (a bound on a value, when a signal is asserted), state it on a line starting ASSUMES: and cite the lines of the design that support it. The reference testbench does not know your assumption, so the rewrite must still match the original on the testbench's stimulus, which does not respect it.
  • Scope. Change only what the rewrite onto the block needs.
Check
  • Every claim the plan makes about the design (who reads a value, a register's reset or enable, widths, cited lines, ASSUMES lines) against the source.
  • That the mapping as described keeps the same behavior.
  • That the plan states everything the planner's output section requires, listed below.

Do not judge cost or style; measurement decides cost.

The plan

I'll start by reading the design source around the candidate regions.

TIER: T2
REGIONS: bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2874$348, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2875$349, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2876$350, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2877$351, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2878$352, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2879$353, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2880$354, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2881$355, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2882$356
SHARED_REDUCTION: the three sums in bilinearintrp: r (i1r+i2r+i3r), g (i1g+i2g+i3g), b (i1b+i2b+i3b) — two blocks feed each sum
BLOCKS: 6
MAPPING:

  • w is a new combinational wire in bilinearintrp: wire [7:0] w = 8'b11111111 - u - v; (exactly the RHS of line 2862, 8-bit, same wrap). u, v stay as they are: the combinational reg [7:0] of the always @(selectuv or ...) case at lines 2818-2858.
  • Block R_UV: ax={10'b0,u}, ay={12'b0,ru}, bx={10'b0,v}, by={12'b0,rv}, chainin=37'b0, result=r_uv[36:0], chainout unconnected.
  • Block R_W: ax={10'b0,w}, ay={12'b0,rw}, bx=18'b0, by=19'b0, chainin=37'b0, result=r_w[36:0], chainout unconnected.
  • Block G_UV: ax={10'b0,u}, ay={12'b0,gu}, bx={10'b0,v}, by={12'b0,gv}, chainin=37'b0, result=g_uv, chainout unconnected.
  • Block G_W: ax={10'b0,w}, ay={12'b0,gw}, bx=18'b0, by=19'b0, chainin=37'b0, result=g_w, chainout unconnected.
  • Block B_UV: ax={10'b0,u}, ay={12'b0,bu}, bx={10'b0,v}, by={12'b0,bv}, chainin=37'b0, result=b_uv, chainout unconnected.
  • Block B_W: ax={10'b0,w}, ay={12'b0,bw}, bx=18'b0, by=19'b0, chainin=37'b0, result=b_w, chainout unconnected.
  • Every block: clk=clk, reset=1'b0, mode_sigs=12'b0.
  • Outputs: assign r = r_uv[6:0] + r_w[6:0]; assign g = g_uv[6:0] + g_w[6:0]; assign b = b_uv[6:0] + b_w[6:0];

PLAN:

What is being replaced (lines 2860-2886). The clocked block holds two register stages: stage 1 latches the muxed u,v, the value 8'hFF-u-v, and the nine colour weights (ul,vl,wl,rul..bwl); stage 2 latches the nine products (i1r..i3b, each an exact 8×7 → 15-bit product). r,g,b are the combinational 3-product sums truncated to 7 bits. All of ul,vl,wl,rul..bwl,i1r..i3b are local regs read only at lines 2874-2886 (verified by grep over the whole file; the ul/vl at lines 2706-2756 belong to linearmap, a different module), so nothing outside reads a value that moves inside a block — the visibility rule is satisfied.

All nine candidates are converted; none is left. Each colour's 3-product chain becomes two blocks: one block takes the u and v products, the second takes the w product alone with bx=by=0. The two 37-bit results are added in 7 bits outside, which is all r,g,b keep.

Why two blocks per colour and not one chained pair. Chaining would be the natural fit for a 3-product sum, but it does not keep the module's latency. chainout is combinational from the upstream block's input registers, so it is valid one cycle after that block's operands are applied; the downstream block then needs two more cycles from its chainin register to result — three cycles in total, while the original module produces r two cycles after the inputs are sampled. There is no earlier tap to steal a cycle from (u,v are combinational from the module's ports) and the module's output may not be delayed, so chaining is not usable here. Every block therefore runs standalone with chainin tied to 0 and chainout left unconnected (it has no legal reader).

Why not the cheaper 3-block variant. Three blocks (the u/v pair per colour) plus three surviving soft 8×7 multipliers and their wl/rwl/gwl/bwl and i3r/i3g/i3b registers would cost fewer DSP tiles, but it leaves the module's longest register-to-register path — a multiplier in soft logic — exactly as it is in the original, so it can only win on area and never on delay. The 6-block version removes all nine multipliers and all 222 flip-flops of the two register stages from the fabric, leaving only the 6:1 u/v mux, one 8-bit subtract and three 7-bit adds in CLBs; the module's register-to-register path becomes the block's 2.219 ns. Since the objective is area × delay over the whole design, the full conversion is the better bet. 6 of 132 DSP tiles are used, well inside the budget.

Register stages, per converted region (all nine are identical).

  • Before: 2 stages from the module's inputs to the sum — stage 1 = ul/vl/wl and the weight registers (lines 2862-2873), stage 2 = the product registers i1r..i3b (lines 2874-2882), then the sum is combinational into r/g/b.
  • After: 2 stages — stage 1 = the block's operand registers (they take the role of ul/vl/wl and the weight registers), stage 2 = the block's result register (it takes the role of the product registers, with the partial sum of two products now formed inside), then the 7-bit add is combinational into r/g/b. Operands applied before edge k reach result after edge k+1 in both the original and the rewrite, so r,g,b change on exactly the same cycles.

Cycle-exact argument. Let the module's inputs be sampled at edge n-1. Original: ul,rul hold u,ru of that sample during (n-1,n); at edge n i1r = u·ru, i2r = v·rv, i3r = w·rw, so r during (n,n+1) is their sum mod 128. Rewrite: each block's operand registers sample the same combinational u,v,w,ru..bw at edge n-1 and load result at edge n, giving r_uv = u·ru + v·rv and r_w = w·rw over the same interval; r_uv[6:0] + r_w[6:0] equals (i1r+i2r+i3r) mod 128. No wrap concerns: each product is at most 255·127 = 32385 and a block's sum at most 64770, far below 2^37, so the block's modulo-2^37 result is the exact sum, and truncating each result to 7 bits before adding is exact modulo 128.

Reset and power-up. bilinearintrp has no reset port and the testbench drives none, so every block's reset is tied to 1'b0 — there is no design reset whose behaviour has to be reproduced. The blocks' registers are X for the first two clock edges; the testbench's 8-cycle all-zero lead-in ends well after that, and with zero inputs both the original and the rewrite read 0 on r,g,b by then. No register the rewrite adds is relied on at power-up (none is added: the only new logic is combinational).

Edits for the editor, inside module bilinearintrp only.

  1. Delete the reg declarations at lines 2796-2816 (ul,vl,wl,i1b..i3r,rul..bwl). Keep reg [7:0] u; reg [7:0] v; (2794-2795) and the combinational always at 2818-2858 unchanged.
  2. Delete the whole always @(posedge clk) block at lines 2860-2883; it becomes empty.
  3. Add wire [7:0] w = 8'b11111111 - u - v; and six 37-bit wires r_uv, r_w, g_uv, g_w, b_uv, b_w.
  4. Instantiate the six int_sop_2 blocks with the port connections in MAPPING, by name, connecting every input port (clk, reset, mode_sigs, ax, ay, bx, by, chainin) and leaving chainout unconnected.
  5. Replace lines 2884-2886 with the three 7-bit adds in MAPPING.
    No other module and nothing outside bilinearintrp changes.

DESIGN_CHANGE: If bilinearintrp were allowed one extra cycle of latency on r,g,b (or if its u/v/weight inputs arrived one register stage earlier from resultrecieve/rgsramcontroller), each colour's three products would fit two blocks joined by chainout→chainin, removing the three soft 7-bit output adders and putting the whole reduction inside the DSP column; the block count would stay 6.

A candidate that does not match is not measured. The testbench (tb_equiv_bilin.v) and the block's behavioural model (int_sop_2.v) are in sim_reference/.

4	// chainout[37] out. mode_sigs is not read.
5	// Registers (the arch's annotation read literally, decision 2026-10-16): every input is registered
6	// at the block's entry at one clock edge (ax, ay, bx, by and chainin alike); the products
7	// and their sum are formed from those registers and loaded into result at the next edge.
8	// Cycles: every operand and chainin to result 2. chainout is combinational from the input

10	// chainin to chainout 1).
11	// Reset: synchronous, active high (2026-10-17: the arch's annotation does not distinguish a reset
12	// register from a clear sampled by the registers themselves): at a clock edge that samples it high
13	// every register of the block (operand registers, chainin register, result) is cleared to 0; the block
14	// loads again at the next edge that samples it low. No register has an initial value: every one is
15	// X until the first clear.
16	module int_sop_2 (

18	    input reset,

25	);

34	    wire [36:0] prod_b = bx_reg * by_reg;
35	    wire [36:0] sum    = prod_a + prod_b + chainin_reg;          // modulo 2^37

prompt
Task

Edit raygentop.v in this directory to carry out the approved plan below: rewrite the regions it names onto the hard block described next. If a step cannot be done as the plan says, stop and say which step and why; do not improvise a different rewrite.

The block
  • primitive to instantiate: int_sop_2 (precision INT19)
  • computes: out = ax*ay + bx*by + chainin
  • input ports: clk[1] (clock), reset[1], mode_sigs[12], ax[18], ay[19], bx[18], by[19], chainin[37]
  • output ports: result[37], chainout[37]
  • arithmetic: the block reads each operand port as an unsigned number, and its result is unsigned. An operand narrower than its port is zero-extended to the port width. The result wraps at 37 bits: a value past the result width keeps its low 37 bits (modulo 2^37), with no saturation and no flag.
  • registers: on clk, the block registers its operands (ax, ay, bx, by) at one clock edge and result at the next, with the arithmetic between the two registers: operands applied before clock edge k appear in result after edge k+1, 2 cycles later. The block has no clock enable and no path around either register. chainin is registered at the block's entry like an operand and reaches result 2 cycles after it is applied. chainout is combinational from the input registers: it carries the value result loads at the next clock edge unless that edge clears it, one cycle before result shows it.
  • reset: reset is synchronous and active high: at a clock edge that samples it high it clears every register of the block (the operand registers, the chainin register and the result register) to 0, and the block loads again at the next edge that samples it low.
  • power-up: the block's registers have no initial value. Each loads its input at every clock edge where reset is low, so, for a block whose inputs, chainin included, are defined, the result is defined from the second edge after power-up, with or without a reset. A rewrite may not rely on the result before then.
  • tie-off: chainin = 0 when the block is not chained (a dedicated chain input: only another block's chainout or a constant may drive it); mode_sigs = 0 always (the architecture gives the port no meaning for this mode).
  • connections: connect every input port, by the port names above; a product-term port the rewrite does not use is tied to 0. chainout reaches only the next block's chainin, through a dedicated connection to the block directly below it in the column; it has no connection to general routing, so nothing else may read it.
The block on this architecture

These are read from the architecture file.

element on a pathns
into the block: a path ends at the block's input register (setup)0.019
inside the block: input register clock-to-Q + delay + output register setup0.060 + 2.140 + 0.019 = 2.219
out of the block: a path starts at the output register of result (clock-to-Q)0.060
chainout: input register clock-to-Q + delay, to the output pin0.060 + 2.140 = 2.200
soft flip-flop, clock to output0.060
  • Blocks in series are separate stages. A path through chainout starts at an input register and continues, in the same stage, into what it drives.
  • A block of this mode takes a DSP tile alone.
  • The block has chain ports: a block's chainout can drive the next block's chainin directly, so a sum can continue from block to block.
  • The device has 132 DSP tiles.
  • One DSP tile's area is 253779 minimum-width transistor areas and one CLB's 27905.
The design
  • Regions in the approved plan: bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2874$348, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2875$349, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2876$350, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2877$351, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2878$352, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2879$353, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2880$354, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2881$355, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2882$356. The plan names their shared sum as 'the three sums in bilinearintrp: r (i1r+i2r+i3r), g (i1g+i2g+i3g), b (i1b+i2b+i3b) — two blocks feed each sum'. In the design their products are registered 1 stage(s) before the sum.
Correctness: these must hold
  • Same behavior. The rewritten design must produce the same outputs as the original on every cycle, with every register of the original starting at 0. Do not change what the design computes. Logic added only to reproduce it (recomputing a value, deriving the block's reset, correcting a block's result) is allowed.
  • Visibility. Only what the block's output ports carry is visible outside it. A value read by anything else (another expression, a module port, another module) cannot go inside a block: keep it outside, or recompute it. Check the source for every reader.
  • Terms. Keep every term of the original arithmetic. A term with no matching block port is computed outside the block. A port the design ties to a constant may be tied the same way on the block.
  • Registers and reset. The block's registers, if it has any, behave as the contract states. A design register may become a block register only if that keeps its behavior. Drive the block's reset so that "same behavior" holds after every reset, not only at start-up. Registers the rewrite adds start unknown; do not rely on their initial value.
  • Assumptions. If the rewrite is correct only under an assumption about the design's inputs or use (a bound on a value, when a signal is asserted), state it on a line starting ASSUMES: and cite the lines of the design that support it. The reference testbench does not know your assumption, so the rewrite must still match the original on the testbench's stimulus, which does not respect it.
  • Scope. Change only what the rewrite onto the block needs.
How to work
  • After each edit, run bash check.sh raygentop.v and read its output. If it fails, fix the RTL and run it again.
  • check.sh reads the architecture's declaration of every hard block. Instantiate the block by its name and do not declare it in the design: a design that defines a module of the block's name fails the check.
  • Do not run place-and-route or any VTR command; that is done after you finish.
  • Stop when check.sh passes and its cell census shows the block instances the plan calls for.
Output
  • State what you changed, region by region, and stop.
  • Your edit is checked automatically, and this reply is read by the framework, not answered by a person, so do not end with a question or an offer.
The approved plan

I'll start by reading the design source around the candidate regions.

TIER: T2
REGIONS: bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2874$348, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2875$349, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2876$350, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2877$351, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2878$352, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2879$353, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2880$354, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2881$355, bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2882$356
SHARED_REDUCTION: the three sums in bilinearintrp: r (i1r+i2r+i3r), g (i1g+i2g+i3g), b (i1b+i2b+i3b) — two blocks feed each sum
BLOCKS: 6
MAPPING:

  • w is a new combinational wire in bilinearintrp: wire [7:0] w = 8'b11111111 - u - v; (exactly the RHS of line 2862, 8-bit, same wrap). u, v stay as they are: the combinational reg [7:0] of the always @(selectuv or ...) case at lines 2818-2858.
  • Block R_UV: ax={10'b0,u}, ay={12'b0,ru}, bx={10'b0,v}, by={12'b0,rv}, chainin=37'b0, result=r_uv[36:0], chainout unconnected.
  • Block R_W: ax={10'b0,w}, ay={12'b0,rw}, bx=18'b0, by=19'b0, chainin=37'b0, result=r_w[36:0], chainout unconnected.
  • Block G_UV: ax={10'b0,u}, ay={12'b0,gu}, bx={10'b0,v}, by={12'b0,gv}, chainin=37'b0, result=g_uv, chainout unconnected.
  • Block G_W: ax={10'b0,w}, ay={12'b0,gw}, bx=18'b0, by=19'b0, chainin=37'b0, result=g_w, chainout unconnected.
  • Block B_UV: ax={10'b0,u}, ay={12'b0,bu}, bx={10'b0,v}, by={12'b0,bv}, chainin=37'b0, result=b_uv, chainout unconnected.
  • Block B_W: ax={10'b0,w}, ay={12'b0,bw}, bx=18'b0, by=19'b0, chainin=37'b0, result=b_w, chainout unconnected.
  • Every block: clk=clk, reset=1'b0, mode_sigs=12'b0.
  • Outputs: assign r = r_uv[6:0] + r_w[6:0]; assign g = g_uv[6:0] + g_w[6:0]; assign b = b_uv[6:0] + b_w[6:0];

PLAN:

What is being replaced (lines 2860-2886). The clocked block holds two register stages: stage 1 latches the muxed u,v, the value 8'hFF-u-v, and the nine colour weights (ul,vl,wl,rul..bwl); stage 2 latches the nine products (i1r..i3b, each an exact 8×7 → 15-bit product). r,g,b are the combinational 3-product sums truncated to 7 bits. All of ul,vl,wl,rul..bwl,i1r..i3b are local regs read only at lines 2874-2886 (verified by grep over the whole file; the ul/vl at lines 2706-2756 belong to linearmap, a different module), so nothing outside reads a value that moves inside a block — the visibility rule is satisfied.

All nine candidates are converted; none is left. Each colour's 3-product chain becomes two blocks: one block takes the u and v products, the second takes the w product alone with bx=by=0. The two 37-bit results are added in 7 bits outside, which is all r,g,b keep.

Why two blocks per colour and not one chained pair. Chaining would be the natural fit for a 3-product sum, but it does not keep the module's latency. chainout is combinational from the upstream block's input registers, so it is valid one cycle after that block's operands are applied; the downstream block then needs two more cycles from its chainin register to result — three cycles in total, while the original module produces r two cycles after the inputs are sampled. There is no earlier tap to steal a cycle from (u,v are combinational from the module's ports) and the module's output may not be delayed, so chaining is not usable here. Every block therefore runs standalone with chainin tied to 0 and chainout left unconnected (it has no legal reader).

Why not the cheaper 3-block variant. Three blocks (the u/v pair per colour) plus three surviving soft 8×7 multipliers and their wl/rwl/gwl/bwl and i3r/i3g/i3b registers would cost fewer DSP tiles, but it leaves the module's longest register-to-register path — a multiplier in soft logic — exactly as it is in the original, so it can only win on area and never on delay. The 6-block version removes all nine multipliers and all 222 flip-flops of the two register stages from the fabric, leaving only the 6:1 u/v mux, one 8-bit subtract and three 7-bit adds in CLBs; the module's register-to-register path becomes the block's 2.219 ns. Since the objective is area × delay over the whole design, the full conversion is the better bet. 6 of 132 DSP tiles are used, well inside the budget.

Register stages, per converted region (all nine are identical).

  • Before: 2 stages from the module's inputs to the sum — stage 1 = ul/vl/wl and the weight registers (lines 2862-2873), stage 2 = the product registers i1r..i3b (lines 2874-2882), then the sum is combinational into r/g/b.
  • After: 2 stages — stage 1 = the block's operand registers (they take the role of ul/vl/wl and the weight registers), stage 2 = the block's result register (it takes the role of the product registers, with the partial sum of two products now formed inside), then the 7-bit add is combinational into r/g/b. Operands applied before edge k reach result after edge k+1 in both the original and the rewrite, so r,g,b change on exactly the same cycles.

Cycle-exact argument. Let the module's inputs be sampled at edge n-1. Original: ul,rul hold u,ru of that sample during (n-1,n); at edge n i1r = u·ru, i2r = v·rv, i3r = w·rw, so r during (n,n+1) is their sum mod 128. Rewrite: each block's operand registers sample the same combinational u,v,w,ru..bw at edge n-1 and load result at edge n, giving r_uv = u·ru + v·rv and r_w = w·rw over the same interval; r_uv[6:0] + r_w[6:0] equals (i1r+i2r+i3r) mod 128. No wrap concerns: each product is at most 255·127 = 32385 and a block's sum at most 64770, far below 2^37, so the block's modulo-2^37 result is the exact sum, and truncating each result to 7 bits before adding is exact modulo 128.

Reset and power-up. bilinearintrp has no reset port and the testbench drives none, so every block's reset is tied to 1'b0 — there is no design reset whose behaviour has to be reproduced. The blocks' registers are X for the first two clock edges; the testbench's 8-cycle all-zero lead-in ends well after that, and with zero inputs both the original and the rewrite read 0 on r,g,b by then. No register the rewrite adds is relied on at power-up (none is added: the only new logic is combinational).

Edits for the editor, inside module bilinearintrp only.

  1. Delete the reg declarations at lines 2796-2816 (ul,vl,wl,i1b..i3r,rul..bwl). Keep reg [7:0] u; reg [7:0] v; (2794-2795) and the combinational always at 2818-2858 unchanged.
  2. Delete the whole always @(posedge clk) block at lines 2860-2883; it becomes empty.
  3. Add wire [7:0] w = 8'b11111111 - u - v; and six 37-bit wires r_uv, r_w, g_uv, g_w, b_uv, b_w.
  4. Instantiate the six int_sop_2 blocks with the port connections in MAPPING, by name, connecting every input port (clk, reset, mode_sigs, ax, ay, bx, by, chainin) and leaving chainout unconnected.
  5. Replace lines 2884-2886 with the three 7-bit adds in MAPPING.
    No other module and nothing outside bilinearintrp changes.
Simulation

When you finish, the framework simulates your edited design against the original with the reference testbench, comparing every output of bilinearintrp on every cycle after the testbench's lead-in phase (its first 8 cycles), on its own copy. The testbench (tb_equiv_bilin.v) and the block's behavioural model (int_sop_2.v) are in sim_reference/; you may read them, but editing them has no effect on the result.

Latency is unchanged: the block's operand registers take the role of ul/vl/wl and the weight registers, its result register the role of i1r..i3b, so r, g, b change on the same cycles as before.

No ASSUMES: line is needed — the rewrite is unconditional.

1	// int_sop_2: result = ax*ay + bx*by + chainin, unsigned, modulo 2^37 (the sum wraps at the
2	// result width).
3	// Ports: clk, reset, mode_sigs[12], ax[18], ay[19], bx[18], by[19], chainin[37] in; result[37],
4	// chainout[37] out. mode_sigs is not read.
5	// Registers (the arch's annotation read literally, decision 2026-10-16): every input is registered
6	// at the block's entry at one clock edge (ax, ay, bx, by and chainin alike); the products
7	// and their sum are formed from those registers and loaded into result at the next edge.
8	// Cycles: every operand and chainin to result 2. chainout is combinational from the input
9	// registers: the sum result loads at the next edge unless that edge clears it (operands and
10	// chainin to chainout 1).
11	// Reset: synchronous, active high (2026-10-17: the arch's annotation does not distinguish a reset
12	// register from a clear sampled by the registers themselves): at a clock edge that samples it high
13	// every register of the block (operand registers, chainin register, result) is cleared to 0; the block
14	// loads again at the next edge that samples it low. No register has an initial value: every one is
15	// X until the first clear.
16	module int_sop_2 (
17	    input clk,
18	    input reset,
19	    input [11:0] mode_sigs,
20	    input [17:0] ax, input [18:0] ay,
21	    input [17:0] bx, input [18:0] by,
22	    input [36:0] chainin,
23	    output reg [36:0] result,
24	    output [36:0] chainout
25	);
26	
27	    // stage 1: the input registers
28	    reg [17:0] ax_reg, bx_reg;
29	    reg [18:0] ay_reg, by_reg;
30	    reg [36:0] chainin_reg;
31	
32	    // combinational middle: the two products and their sum, from the registers
33	    wire [36:0] prod_a = ax_reg * ay_reg;                        // 18x19 -> 37, exact
34	    wire [36:0] prod_b = bx_reg * by_reg;
35	    wire [36:0] sum    = prod_a + prod_b + chainin_reg;          // modulo 2^37
36	
37	    always @(posedge clk) begin
38	        if (reset) begin
39	            result <= 0;
40	            ax_reg <= 0; ay_reg <= 0; bx_reg <= 0; by_reg <= 0; chainin_reg <= 0;
41	        end
42	        else begin
43	            ax_reg <= ax; ay_reg <= ay; bx_reg <= bx; by_reg <= by; chainin_reg <= chainin;
44	            result <= sum;                                       // stage 2
45	        end
46	    end
47	
48	    assign chainout = sum;                                  // the sum of the input registers
49	
50	endmodule

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Inner-loop check: the front end of the real flow, then a cell census. NOT place-and-route.
# usage: bash check.sh <file.v>
# Reads, in the flow's order: blocks.vlib (every hard block of the architecture, declared from its
# models), primitives.vlib (the flow's library of memories, adders and multipliers), then the design;
# then elaborates with the flow's hierarchy check. A module that is not defined, a port name the
# block does not have, or a misspelled block name fails here as it would in the flow. Do not
# declare the target block in the design: blocks.vlib declares it, and a design that defines a module
# of the target block's name fails this check.
# Prints one pass/fail line, then the cell census of the whole design (the last "Number of cells"
# table yosys prints): soft arithmetic as $mul/$add/$sub, registers as $dff*/$sdff*, memories as
# $mem*, muxes as $mux/$pmux, and every instantiated module by name, which is where a hard-block
# instantiation shows up with its instance count.
set -u
f="${1:?usage: check.sh <file.v>}"
here="$(cd "$(dirname "$0")" && pwd)"
lib=""
[ -f "$here/blocks.vlib" ] && lib="read_verilog -lib $here/blocks.vlib; "
[ -f "$here/primitives.vlib" ] && lib="${lib}read_verilog -nomem2reg $here/primitives.vlib; "
target="$(sed -n 's|^// target block: *\([A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_$]*\).*|\1|p' "$here/blocks.vlib" 2>/dev/null | head -1)"
if [ -n "$target" ] && grep -Eq "^[[:space:]]*module[[:space:]]+$target([^A-Za-z0-9_$]|$)" "$f"; then
  echo "FAIL: $f defines module $target; blocks.vlib declares it. Remove the module definition and keep the instances."
  exit 1
fi
out="$(yosys -p "${lib}read_verilog -sv -nolatches $f; hierarchy -check -auto-top -purge_lib; proc; opt; stat" 2>&1)"
rc=$?
if [ $rc -ne 0 ]; then
  echo "FAIL: does not synthesize (yosys exit $rc)"
  echo "$out" | grep -iE 'error|warning' | tail -15
  echo "$out" | tail -10
  exit 1
fi
echo "OK: synthesizes cleanly"
echo "Cell census (yosys stat, whole design):"
echo "$out" | awk '/Number of cells:/{buf=$0 "\n"; on=1; next}
                   on && /^[[:space:]]+[$A-Za-z_][^[:space:]]*[[:space:]]+[0-9]+[[:space:]]*$/ {buf=buf $0 "\n"; next}
                   on {on=0}
                   END{printf "%s", buf}' \
  | grep -E 'Number of cells|^[[:space:]]+(\$(mul|add|sub|mem|memrd|memwr|dff|dffe|sdff|sdffe|adff|adffe|mux|pmux)|[A-Za-z_])' || true

Task

You are the acceptance reviewer. You did not plan or write this edit. Check the edit against the approved plan, the block's contract and the correctness rules.

The block
  • primitive to instantiate: int_sop_2 (precision INT19)
  • computes: out = ax*ay + bx*by + chainin
  • input ports: clk[1] (clock), reset[1], mode_sigs[12], ax[18], ay[19], bx[18], by[19], chainin[37]
  • output ports: result[37], chainout[37]
  • arithmetic: the block reads each operand port as an unsigned number, and its result is unsigned. An operand narrower than its port is zero-extended to the port width. The result wraps at 37 bits: a value past the result width keeps its low 37 bits (modulo 2^37), with no saturation and no flag.
  • registers: on clk, the block registers its operands (ax, ay, bx, by) at one clock edge and result at the next, with the arithmetic between the two registers: operands applied before clock edge k appear in result after edge k+1, 2 cycles later. The block has no clock enable and no path around either register. chainin is registered at the block's entry like an operand and reaches result 2 cycles after it is applied. chainout is combinational from the input registers: it carries the value result loads at the next clock edge unless that edge clears it, one cycle before result shows it.
  • reset: reset is synchronous and active high: at a clock edge that samples it high it clears every register of the block (the operand registers, the chainin register and the result register) to 0, and the block loads again at the next edge that samples it low.
  • power-up: the block's registers have no initial value. Each loads its input at every clock edge where reset is low, so, for a block whose inputs, chainin included, are defined, the result is defined from the second edge after power-up, with or without a reset. A rewrite may not rely on the result before then.
  • tie-off: chainin = 0 when the block is not chained (a dedicated chain input: only another block's chainout or a constant may drive it); mode_sigs = 0 always (the architecture gives the port no meaning for this mode).
  • connections: connect every input port, by the port names above; a product-term port the rewrite does not use is tied to 0. chainout reaches only the next block's chainin, through a dedicated connection to the block directly below it in the column; it has no connection to general routing, so nothing else may read it.
The block on this architecture

  • Blocks in series are separate stages. A path through chainout starts at an input register and continues, in the same stage, into what it drives.
  • A block of this mode takes a DSP tile alone.
  • The block has chain ports: a block's chainout can drive the next block's chainin directly, so a sum can continue from block to block.
  • The device has 132 DSP tiles.
  • One DSP tile's area is 253779 minimum-width transistor areas and one CLB's 27905.
The design

The file raygentop.v is the edited design. The edit is the diff below, taken against the original source.

Correctness: these must hold
  • Same behavior. The rewritten design must produce the same outputs as the original on every cycle, with every register of the original starting at 0. Do not change what the design computes. Logic added only to reproduce it (recomputing a value, deriving the block's reset, correcting a block's result) is allowed.
  • Visibility. Only what the block's output ports carry is visible outside it. A value read by anything else (another expression, a module port, another module) cannot go inside a block: keep it outside, or recompute it. Check the source for every reader.
  • Terms. Keep every term of the original arithmetic. A term with no matching block port is computed outside the block. A port the design ties to a constant may be tied the same way on the block.
  • Registers and reset. The block's registers, if it has any, behave as the contract states. A design register may become a block register only if that keeps its behavior. Drive the block's reset so that "same behavior" holds after every reset, not only at start-up. Registers the rewrite adds start unknown; do not rely on their initial value.
  • Assumptions. If the rewrite is correct only under an assumption about the design's inputs or use (a bound on a value, when a signal is asserted), state it on a line starting ASSUMES: and cite the lines of the design that support it. The reference testbench does not know your assumption, so the rewrite must still match the original on the testbench's stimulus, which does not respect it.
  • Scope. Change only what the rewrite onto the block needs.

The approved plan

  • Outputs: assign r = r_uv[6:0] + r_w[6:0]; assign g = g_uv[6:0] + g_w[6:0]; assign b = b_uv[6:0] + b_w[6:0];

PLAN:

What is being replaced (lines 2860-2886). The clocked block holds two register stages: stage 1 latches the muxed u,v, the value 8'hFF-u-v, and the nine colour weights (ul,vl,wl,rul..bwl); stage 2 latches the nine products (i1r..i3b, each an exact 8×7 → 15-bit product). r,g,b are the combinational 3-product sums truncated to 7 bits. All of ul,vl,wl,rul..bwl,i1r..i3b are local regs read only at lines 2874-2886 (verified by grep over the whole file; the ul/vl at lines 2706-2756 belong to linearmap, a different module), so nothing outside reads a value that moves inside a block — the visibility rule is satisfied.

All nine candidates are converted; none is left. Each colour's 3-product chain becomes two blocks: one block takes the u and v products, the second takes the w product alone with bx=by=0. The two 37-bit results are added in 7 bits outside, which is all r,g,b keep.

Why two blocks per colour and not one chained pair. Chaining would be the natural fit for a 3-product sum, but it does not keep the module's latency. chainout is combinational from the upstream block's input registers, so it is valid one cycle after that block's operands are applied; the downstream block then needs two more cycles from its chainin register to result — three cycles in total, while the original module produces r two cycles after the inputs are sampled. There is no earlier tap to steal a cycle from (u,v are combinational from the module's ports) and the module's output may not be delayed, so chaining is not usable here. Every block therefore runs standalone with chainin tied to 0 and chainout left unconnected (it has no legal reader).

Why not the cheaper 3-block variant. Three blocks (the u/v pair per colour) plus three surviving soft 8×7 multipliers and their wl/rwl/gwl/bwl and i3r/i3g/i3b registers would cost fewer DSP tiles, but it leaves the module's longest register-to-register path — a multiplier in soft logic — exactly as it is in the original, so it can only win on area and never on delay. The 6-block version removes all nine multipliers and all 222 flip-flops of the two register stages from the fabric, leaving only the 6:1 u/v mux, one 8-bit subtract and three 7-bit adds in CLBs; the module's register-to-register path becomes the block's 2.219 ns. Since the objective is area × delay over the whole design, the full conversion is the better bet. 6 of 132 DSP tiles are used, well inside the budget.

Register stages, per converted region (all nine are identical).

  • Before: 2 stages from the module's inputs to the sum — stage 1 = ul/vl/wl and the weight registers (lines 2862-2873), stage 2 = the product registers i1r..i3b (lines 2874-2882), then the sum is combinational into r/g/b.
  • After: 2 stages — stage 1 = the block's operand registers (they take the role of ul/vl/wl and the weight registers), stage 2 = the block's result register (it takes the role of the product registers, with the partial sum of two products now formed inside), then the 7-bit add is combinational into r/g/b. Operands applied before edge k reach result after edge k+1 in both the original and the rewrite, so r,g,b change on exactly the same cycles.

Cycle-exact argument. Let the module's inputs be sampled at edge n-1. Original: ul,rul hold u,ru of that sample during (n-1,n); at edge n i1r = u·ru, i2r = v·rv, i3r = w·rw, so r during (n,n+1) is their sum mod 128. Rewrite: each block's operand registers sample the same combinational u,v,w,ru..bw at edge n-1 and load result at edge n, giving r_uv = u·ru + v·rv and r_w = w·rw over the same interval; r_uv[6:0] + r_w[6:0] equals (i1r+i2r+i3r) mod 128. No wrap concerns: each product is at most 255·127 = 32385 and a block's sum at most 64770, far below 2^37, so the block's modulo-2^37 result is the exact sum, and truncating each result to 7 bits before adding is exact modulo 128.

Reset and power-up. bilinearintrp has no reset port and the testbench drives none, so every block's reset is tied to 1'b0 — there is no design reset whose behaviour has to be reproduced. The blocks' registers are X for the first two clock edges; the testbench's 8-cycle all-zero lead-in ends well after that, and with zero inputs both the original and the rewrite read 0 on r,g,b by then. No register the rewrite adds is relied on at power-up (none is added: the only new logic is combinational).

Edits for the editor, inside module bilinearintrp only.

  1. Delete the reg declarations at lines 2796-2816 (ul,vl,wl,i1b..i3r,rul..bwl). Keep reg [7:0] u; reg [7:0] v; (2794-2795) and the combinational always at 2818-2858 unchanged.
  2. Delete the whole always @(posedge clk) block at lines 2860-2883; it becomes empty.
  3. Add wire [7:0] w = 8'b11111111 - u - v; and six 37-bit wires r_uv, r_w, g_uv, g_w, b_uv, b_w.
  4. Instantiate the six int_sop_2 blocks with the port connections in MAPPING, by name, connecting every input port (clk, reset, mode_sigs, ax, ay, bx, by, chainin) and leaving chainout unconnected.
  5. Replace lines 2884-2886 with the three 7-bit adds in MAPPING.
    No other module and nothing outside bilinearintrp changes.

DESIGN_CHANGE: If bilinearintrp were allowed one extra cycle of latency on r,g,b (or if its u/v/weight inputs arrived one register stage earlier from resultrecieve/rgsramcontroller), each colour's three products would fit two blocks joined by chainout→chainin, removing the three soft 7-bit output adders and putting the whole reduction inside the DSP column; the block count would stay 6.

ACCEPT

The edit matches the approved plan exactly, and the correctness rules hold.

Plan conformance. All nine candidate regions (the nine ul/vl/wl × weight products at original lines 2874-2882) are converted into six int_sop_2 instances, two per colour, with the MAPPING port connections reproduced verbatim (raygentop.v:2849-2883). The new wire[7:0] w = 8'b11111111 - u - v; (raygentop.v:2798) is the 8-bit RHS of the original wl assignment with the same wrap; u/v and the combinational always @(selectuv or ...) case (raygentop.v:2804-2844) are untouched; the two register stages and all 22 removed regs are gone; the outputs are the three 7-bit adds (raygentop.v:2885-2887). Nothing outside bilinearintrp changed — the harness subtree check in sim_run.log:3 confirms this, and bilinearintrp is instantiated once (raygentop.v:2012), so the cost is 6 of 132 DSP tiles.

Ports. Every input port of every block is connected by name: clk, reset(1'b0), mode_sigs(12'b0), ax/ay/bx/by with correct widths ({10'b0,u} = 18, {12'b0,ru} = 19, unused product term tied to 18'b0/19'b0 on the _w blocks), chainin(37'b0) as a constant tie-off. result drives the 37-bit local wires; chainout is left unconnected, which is the only legal choice here since nothing but another block's chainin may read it.

Same behavior. Original: stage 1 holds u,v,8'hFF-u-v and the nine 7-bit weights; stage 2 holds the products; r/g/b are combinational sums truncated to 7 bits. Rewrite: the block's operand registers take stage 1 and the result register takes stage 2, so a value applied before edge k reaches r/g/b after edge k+1 in both — confirmed by the impulse probe (sim_run.log:24-27, ref and dut both first respond at +2). Arithmetically each product is at most 255·127 = 32385 and each block's sum at most 64770, far under 2^37, so the block's modulo-2^37 result is exact; taking [6:0] of each result and adding is exact modulo 128, which is all the 7-bit r/g/b keep, and matches the original's 15-bit sum truncated to 7 bits.

Visibility. The deleted regs (ul,vl,wl,rul..bwl,i1r..i3b) have no remaining reader anywhere in the file; the only surviving ul/vl are at lines 2706-2756, inside the preceding module linearmap (which ends at line 2761), a different declaration. The values moved inside the blocks are read only through result.

Registers and reset. bilinearintrp has no reset port, so reset tied to 1'b0 reproduces the original's reset behaviour (there is none); the reset policy of the run is none (sim_run.log:10). No register is added by the rewrite — the new logic is purely combinational — so nothing depends on an added register's initial value. The blocks are X for the first two edges, which the testbench's 8-cycle lead-in covers (x_on_dut_only: 0). No ASSUMES: line is needed, and none is claimed.

 "file_path": "/work/sim_reference/int_sop_2.v"

output
1	// int_sop_2: result = ax*ay + bx*by + chainin, unsigned, modulo 2^37 (the sum wraps at the
2	// result width).
3	// Ports: clk, reset, mode_sigs[12], ax[18], ay[19], bx[18], by[19], chainin[37] in; result[37],
4	// chainout[37] out. mode_sigs is not read.
5	// Registers (the arch's annotation read literally, decision 2026-10-16): every input is registered
6	// at the block's entry at one clock edge (ax, ay, bx, by and chainin alike); the products
7	// and their sum are formed from those registers and loaded into result at the next edge.
8	// Cycles: every operand and chainin to result 2. chainout is combinational from the input
9	// registers: the sum result loads at the next edge unless that edge clears it (operands and
10	// chainin to chainout 1).
11	// Reset: synchronous, active high (2026-10-17: the arch's annotation does not distinguish a reset
12	// register from a clear sampled by the registers themselves): at a clock edge that samples it high
13	// every register of the block (operand registers, chainin register, result) is cleared to 0; the block
14	// loads again at the next edge that samples it low. No register has an initial value: every one is
15	// X until the first clear.
16	module int_sop_2 (
17	    input clk,
18	    input reset,
19	    input [11:0] mode_sigs,
20	    input [17:0] ax, input [18:0] ay,
21	    input [17:0] bx, input [18:0] by,
22	    input [36:0] chainin,
23	    output reg [36:0] result,
24	    output [36:0] chainout
25	);
26	
27	    // stage 1: the input registers
28	    reg [17:0] ax_reg, bx_reg;
29	    reg [18:0] ay_reg, by_reg;
30	    reg [36:0] chainin_reg;
31	
32	    // combinational middle: the two products and their sum, from the registers
33	    wire [36:0] prod_a = ax_reg * ay_reg;                        // 18x19 -> 37, exact
34	    wire [36:0] prod_b = bx_reg * by_reg;
35	    wire [36:0] sum    = prod_a + prod_b + chainin_reg;          // modulo 2^37
36	
37	    always @(posedge clk) begin
38	        if (reset) begin
39	            result <= 0;
40	            ax_reg <= 0; ay_reg <= 0; bx_reg <= 0; by_reg <= 0; chainin_reg <= 0;
41	        end
42	        else begin
43	            ax_reg <= ax; ay_reg <= ay; bx_reg <= bx; by_reg <= by; chainin_reg <= chainin;
44	            result <= sum;                                       // stage 2
45	        end
46	    end
47	
48	    assign chainout = sum;                                  // the sum of the input registers
49	
50	endmodule
51

prompt

3 paragraph breaks restored by the export, where the recorder joined text blocks with no separator.

Task

You are the planner. Read the design source, raygentop.v, and decide which of the candidate arithmetic regions listed below to rewrite onto the hard block described next, or that none should be rewritten. Write a plan for the editor, who carries it out; do not edit any file.

The block
  • primitive to instantiate: int_sop_2 (precision INT19)
  • computes: out = ax*ay + bx*by + chainin
  • input ports: clk[1] (clock), reset[1], mode_sigs[12], ax[18], ay[19], bx[18], by[19], chainin[37]
  • output ports: result[37], chainout[37]
  • arithmetic: the block reads each operand port as an unsigned number, and its result is unsigned. An operand narrower than its port is zero-extended to the port width. The result wraps at 37 bits: a value past the result width keeps its low 37 bits (modulo 2^37), with no saturation and no flag.
  • registers: on clk, the block registers its operands (ax, ay, bx, by) at one clock edge and result at the next, with the arithmetic between the two registers: operands applied before clock edge k appear in result after edge k+1, 2 cycles later. The block has no clock enable and no path around either register. chainin is registered at the block's entry like an operand and reaches result 2 cycles after it is applied. chainout is combinational from the input registers: it carries the value result loads at the next clock edge unless that edge clears it, one cycle before result shows it.
  • reset: reset is synchronous and active high: at a clock edge that samples it high it clears every register of the block (the operand registers, the chainin register and the result register) to 0, and the block loads again at the next edge that samples it low.
  • power-up: the block's registers have no initial value. Each loads its input at every clock edge where reset is low, so, for a block whose inputs, chainin included, are defined, the result is defined from the second edge after power-up, with or without a reset. A rewrite may not rely on the result before then.
  • tie-off: chainin = 0 when the block is not chained (a dedicated chain input: only another block's chainout or a constant may drive it); mode_sigs = 0 always (the architecture gives the port no meaning for this mode).
  • connections: connect every input port, by the port names above; a product-term port the rewrite does not use is tied to 0. chainout reaches only the next block's chainin, through a dedicated connection to the block directly below it in the column; it has no connection to general routing, so nothing else may read it.
The block on this architecture

These are read from the architecture file.

element on a pathns
into the block: a path ends at the block's input register (setup)0.019
inside the block: input register clock-to-Q + delay + output register setup0.060 + 2.140 + 0.019 = 2.219
out of the block: a path starts at the output register of result (clock-to-Q)0.060
chainout: input register clock-to-Q + delay, to the output pin0.060 + 2.140 = 2.200
soft flip-flop, clock to output0.060
  • Blocks in series are separate stages. A path through chainout starts at an input register and continues, in the same stage, into what it drives.
  • A block of this mode takes a DSP tile alone.
  • The block has chain ports: a block's chainout can drive the next block's chainin directly, so a sum can continue from block to block.
  • The device has 132 DSP tiles.
  • One DSP tile's area is 253779 minimum-width transistor areas and one CLB's 27905.
The design
Candidate regions

An id names a product by its module and its multiply. One that ends in (sum_in=<module>) is a product whose sum sits in that other module: the product leaves its own module through a port and is added there, so the arithmetic to convert spans both. A #1, #2 after an id separates instances of the same product that sit in sums of different shapes.

In every row, operation: sop.
In every row, instances: 1.
In every row, register stages between the products and their sum: products registered 1 stage(s) before the sum.
In every row, products in the whole sum this one belongs to: 3 products in one chain of adders.

id

bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2875$349
bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2876$350
bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2877$351
bilinearintrp.$mul$raygentop.v:2878$352

Correctness: these must hold
  • Same behavior. The rewritten design must produce the same outputs as the original on every cycle, with every register of the original starting at 0. Do not change what the design computes. Logic added only to reproduce it (recomputing a value, deriving the block's reset, correcting a block's result) is allowed.
  • Visibility. Only what the block's output ports carry is visible outside it. A value read by anything else (another expression, a module port, another module) cannot go inside a block: keep it outside, or recompute it. Check the source for every reader.
  • Terms. Keep every term of the original arithmetic. A term with no matching block port is computed outside the block. A port the design ties to a constant may be tied the same way on the block.
  • Registers and reset. The block's registers, if it has any, behave as the contract states. A design register may become a block register only if that keeps its behavior. Drive the block's reset so that "same behavior" holds after every reset, not only at start-up. Registers the rewrite adds start unknown; do not rely on their initial value.
  • Assumptions. If the rewrite is correct only under an assumption about the design's inputs or use (a bound on a value, when a signal is asserted), state it on a line starting ASSUMES: and cite the lines of the design that support it. The reference testbench does not know your assumption, so the rewrite must still match the original on the testbench's stimulus, which does not respect it.
  • Scope. Change only what the rewrite onto the block needs.
Objective

Minimize area × delay against the original design. Among rewrites that lower it, choose the one that lowers it most.

Output
Tiers
  • T1: The block replaces the region's arithmetic as it stands: the same operations on the same operands, now inside the block.
  • T2: The region's arithmetic is regrouped to fit the block (products regrouped into the block's sums, registers moved), computing the same values.
  • NONE: You propose no rewrite this time. The plan states why, from what you were given: no correct rewrite, or none expected to lower area × delay, or the best result so far is the best available. The run then ends and keeps the best result so far if its median over every seed is below the original's, and the original design otherwise (also when no rewrite has beaten it).
Header

Write exactly this header, each key at the start of its own line, then the plan:

TIER: <exactly one of T1, T2, NONE>
REGIONS: <the converted candidate ids, comma-separated; NONE when TIER is NONE>
SHARED_REDUCTION: <the signal or module the converted products are summed in; NONE for a single region>
BLOCKS: <how many blocks the rewrite instantiates, one integer>
MAPPING: <which design signals go to which block ports>
PLAN: <the plan, stating everything listed below>

What the plan must state
  • Every candidate region, converted or left, with the reason each is left; candidates that share a reason may be grouped.
  • How many blocks the rewrite adds.
  • For each converted region, the register stages from its operands to its result, before and after the rewrite.
Output rules
  • Write nothing before the TIER line; read the source first.
  • Your reply is read by the framework, not answered by a person, so do not end with a question or an offer.
  • If a change to the design itself, not to the rewrite, would let more of it use the block, you may say so on a line starting DESIGN_CHANGE:. It is reported to the user and does not change your decision.
